[[file:~/.claude/plans/hazy-moseying-sky.md][Plan]]

The watermark advances only for *applied* items — deliberately, and
rightly: an item skipped this pass is usually one deferred, not one
refused. But the keymap offers =m=/=u=/=t=/=e=/=RET=/=x=/=g=/=?= and no
dismissal, so an item you will never apply has no exit. It reappears at
every review, forever.

*Not hypothetical as of 2026-08-11:* three permanent residents after the
first post-cutover pass —

- the =dead-beef= phantom clock
  ([[id:5e2a1c04-7b3f-4d21-9c88-2f6e0a91b7d3][5e2a1c04]]), which cannot
  ever apply because its =:ID:= does not resolve;
- two pre-cutover =STALE= state events
  (=8f2c1a90= =TODO -> DOING= 13:09, =feba67eb= =NEXT -> DOING= 13:29)
  whose transitions the tools already performed live. Confirming them
  would write =DOING -> DOING= no-ops, the shape behind
  [[id:3d93021d-1d8a-4657-bc5c-77734f91fb9d][3d93021d]].

Today the only ways out are hand-editing a file the design calls
append-only, or forging watermark entries. Both are worse than the
problem.

*Design question, not just a missing key.* Dismissal and deferral are
different intents and the watermark cannot currently tell them apart —
it records "applied," which is a third thing again. Options worth
weighing: a separate =.dismissed= set beside =.applied=; a =kind= the
reader skips; or a reason-carrying dismissal, since "already applied
live pre-cutover" and "this event should never have existed" want
different treatment at any later audit. The pre-cutover stales are also
self-limiting — they stop being generated at the cutover — which argues
against over-engineering for them specifically.

*Outcome (2026-08-12, commits =2ce42fc= and =0942282=).* Chose the
reason-carrying dismissal, stored as a =dismissed= map (timestamp ->
reason) *inside* the existing =.applied= file. Rejected a separate
=.dismissed= file — it doubles the read and write paths and the
atomicity surface for no gain, since both facts concern one session's
queue and are always read together — and rejected a =kind= the reader
skips, which would mean writing into the queue the design keeps
append-only. Deferral needs no new spelling: it is still simply not
marking.

The load-bearing piece is not the map but the *writer*.
=--queue-mark-applied= previously serialized only its own key, so once
=dismissed= existed in the file any apply would have erased every
dismissal — and that symptom reads as "dismissal is broken" rather than
"apply clobbered it." Both writers now go through one
=--queue-watermark-write= that takes both facts, making the clobber
impossible by construction rather than by remembering. Bound to =d=;
=--queue-events= skips dismissed alongside applied.

*Verified:* =bin/test= 170 -> 175, five new tests, the load-bearing one
being the clobber regression asserted in *both* orders. Then the real
end-to-end pass, by hand: all three permanent residents retired — the
=dead-beef= phantom span and the two pre-cutover =STALE= transitions —
across *two* different sessions' watermark files, which exercised the
per-session write path. Pending items went 4 -> 1, and =5063f096= kept
all 13 of its applied entries alongside its new dismissals, which was
the case most at risk.

*A defect in this feature, found and fixed in the same session
(=0942282=).* Routing through the shared writer with a possibly-empty
applied set produced ="applied":null= — a shape that could not arise
before, since the only previous writer was always called with at least
one entry. Harmless in process, because Elisp spells the empty list, the
empty object and JSON null all as nil and this reader collapses them; a
round-trip test therefore *cannot* catch it. It is not harmless outside:
=d.get("applied", [])= does not fall back when the key is present
holding null, which crashed a real inspection script against a real
watermark file during this session. Empty sets now serialize as =[]= and
={}=, asserted on the bytes rather than the round-trip, and the four
existing files were normalized in place with content verified identical
across the rewrite.

*Left behind, inert:* normalizing iterated every session with a queue
file and so created an empty =386e84cc-….applied= that had not existed.
It reads ={"applied":[],"dismissed":{}}=, which the reader treats
exactly as a missing file. Not cleaned up — the delete was declined.

*** DONE Restore the read-only flag after review-apply clears it :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       45ec3ff6-cde1-42a6-a899-6b11982112cb
:CREATED:  [2026-08-12 Wed 10:00]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-08-12 Wed 11:24]--[2026-08-12 Wed 12:39] =>  1:15
CLOCK: [2026-08-12 Wed 10:02]--[2026-08-12 Wed 10:33] =>  0:31
- State "DONE"       from "DOING"      [2026-08-12 Wed 10:02] \\
  read-only restored via unwind-protect
- <2026-08-12 Wed 10:02>--<2026-08-12 Wed 10:33>
- <2026-08-12 Wed 11:24>--<2026-08-12 Wed 12:39>
:END:

Raised by the user 2026-08-12, right after the first real dismissal
round. =claude-code-ide-org--review-ensure-writable= offers to clear
=buffer-read-only= on every buffer an apply needs to write, and nothing
puts it back. The user sets that flag to guard against their own stray
keystrokes, so a silently-disabled guard is exactly the cost described
in [[id:c8a97d9d-8b13-4b6a-a8b9-1a3f24b5e00b][c8a97d9d]].

*Why clear-then-restore here, when c8a97d9d rejected it.* That heading
weighed clear-then-restore against binding =inhibit-read-only= for the
*MCP tool wrappers* and rejected it on two grounds: a failure window if
a tool errors mid-call, and races between concurrent sessions
interleaving clear/restore. Neither transfers to this path. Apply is
only ever reached from a genuinely interactive command and is never
invoked programmatically — the load-bearing constraint of the whole
queue design — so there is no second session to race. And the failure
window closes with =unwind-protect=, which is the mechanism that
objection did not consider. c8a97d9d's verdict still stands for the
wrappers; this is a different path with different properties, not a
reversal.

Restores only what this command actually cleared, so a buffer the user
had already made writable is left alone.

*Outcome (2026-08-12, commit =de1e7e6=).*
=--review-ensure-writable= now *returns* the buffers it cleared, and
=claude-code-ide-org-review-apply= restores them in an
=unwind-protect=. Returning them rather than re-deriving the list
afterwards is what keeps a buffer the user had already made writable
from being switched to read-only by this command — restoring means
putting back what was borrowed, not imposing a flag that was never
there. The clear happens *outside* the =unwind-protect=, since declining
signals and there is then nothing to put back.

*Verified:* =bin/test= 175 -> 177, two new tests — restored after a
successful apply, and restored after an error thrown mid-apply, which is
precisely the failure window
[[id:c8a97d9d-8b13-4b6a-a8b9-1a3f24b5e00b][c8a97d9d]] named as its
objection. The second test also covers the never-read-only case, where
the buffer must be left writable and no prompt asked.

*Confirmed live on real data, not just in batch.* The user applied three
queued =DONE= transitions into =TODO.org= at 10:02 with the fix loaded.
That apply had to make the buffer writable to land, and =TODO.org= was
read-only again afterwards — with the user confirming they did not
re-set it by hand. That is the whole loop: borrowed, written, returned.

*** DONE Two display defects in the review buffer's rendering of a failed item :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       e3f70e61-6616-4c57-81e8-e350ffd5824c
:CREATED:  [2026-08-11 Tue 16:32]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "DONE"       from "DOING"      [2026-08-12 Wed 16:13] \\
  one defect fixed, one was never real
- State "DOING"      from "TODO"       [2026-08-12 Wed 16:13] \\
  batched with the interaction fixes
:END:

Both seen 2026-08-11, on the one =dead-beef= item that will ever exist
once [[id:5e2a1c04-7b3f-4d21-9c88-2f6e0a91b7d3][5e2a1c04]]'s fix ships.
Recording them because the reproducer is about to become
unconstructible by ordinary means.

The good news first: an unresolvable =:ID:= does not crash the render.
The error string is substituted where the heading title goes, which is
the right instinct. But:

1. *Elisp escaping leaks into the display.* The title line reads

   : Error: no org heading found with :ID: \"00000000-dead-beef-...\

   with literal backslash-quotes and a dangling trailing =\=. The error
   string is being inserted with its printed representation intact
   rather than as display text.

2. *The note renders twice* on the clock line — once inside the
   suggested-interval clause and again after it:

   : - <...15:12>--<...15:16> negative test, bogus id must not enqueue  (suggested)   negative test, bogus id must not enqueue

Neither is dangerous, and neither is worth much on its own. Worth fixing
together with whatever touches the renderer next, since a title slot
that can hold an arbitrary error string is a general case, not one tied
to this event: any =--at-id= failure at render time takes the same path.

*Outcome, 2026-08-12, batched with
[[id:d409390b-6624-4820-82bb-730d763962fd][d409390b]]. One of the two was
real; the other never happened.*

*(2) The double note was real and is fixed.*
=--review-format-annotation= already ends with the note, because the
note is part of the =:LOGBOOK:= line it produces, and
=--review-describe= appended it a second time. Dropped from the clock
branch.

*(1) The escaping leak is not reproducible, and there was never a path
for it.* =--at-id= builds its message with =format= and =%s=, and
=--review-render= substitutes it with =%s= — neither prints an escaped
representation. Reconstructed the exact case (an item on an
unresolvable =:ID:=, rendered into a review buffer) and read the raw
buffer text: =Error: no org heading found with :ID:
"00000000-dead-beef-0000-000000000000"=, real quotes, no backslash
anywhere. *The =\"= and dangling =\= in the report above came from how
it was transcribed into this heading*, not from the renderer.

Worth stating plainly rather than quietly dropping: this heading asked
for a fix to something that was working. The reproducer was described as
about to become unconstructible, which is exactly why it was recorded —
and also why it was never checked before being written down. Both
behaviors are now pinned by tests, including one asserting the rendered
title contains no backslash, so a future =%S= cannot introduce the
defect this heading believed it had found.
*** DONE Queue attribution is session-scoped, so concurrent subagents lose intervals :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       0d789b68-faca-4911-868a-fdd70acbfd40
:CREATED:  [2026-08-12 Wed 17:45]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "DONE"       from "DOING"      [2026-08-13 Thu 09:46] \\
  per-lane attribution; both agents pair correctly
- State "DOING"      from "NEXT"       [2026-08-13 Thu 09:44] \\
  implementing per-agent attribution lanes
- State "NEXT"       from "TODO"       [2026-08-13 Thu 09:43] \\
  re-promoted after auto-demotion side effect
- State "NEXT"       from              [2026-08-12 Wed 17:46] \\
  reproducible attribution bug, fix is small
- State "TODO"       from "NEXT"       [2026-08-12 Wed 22:39] \\
  Auto-demoted: superseded by sibling [[id:7387f97c-6c3e-4667-8851-203af024fdd9][Apply writes headless state notes -- RETRACTED, a grep artifact]] becoming NEXT.
:END:

Found 2026-08-12 by deliberately running two subagents concurrently to
exercise =agent_id=, which until that moment had never been populated
in 347 events. Both halves of the result matter: the field works, and
the code that consumes it is defeated upstream.

=--queue-events-by-id= resolves which heading an id-less event belongs
to by walking each *session's* stream with a single =current= variable
(config.el:2503-2510). Subagents share their parent's =session_id=, so
two concurrent agents interleave into one walk and clobber each other's
=current=. =org_clock_out= sends no =id= at all (config.el:359), so it
depends entirely on that variable.

Measured, not reasoned:

: 17:40:01  clock_in  e51d6ba1  agent=a8a8  -> current = e51d6ba1
: 17:40:20  clock_in  7771fc63  agent=ae39  -> current = 7771fc63  (clobbers)
: 17:42:20  clock_out  id=nil   agent=ae39  -> resolves 7771fc63, current = nil
: 17:46:  clock_out  id=nil   agent=a8a8  -> current already nil -> NIL BUCKET

=--review-items-from-queue= skips the nil bucket outright (=(when id
...)=, config.el:2515), so in a *single* review round =a8a8='s
=clock_out= is dropped and its =clock_in= is left orphaned in
=e51d6ba1='s group with nothing to close it: =e51d6ba1= yields *0*
clock items, =7771fc63= yields 2.

*Correction, 2026-08-12, from the apply pass that followed.* An
earlier draft of this heading stated the interval was lost outright.
It was not. Both intervals landed correctly, because the human applied
in *two rounds*: round one consumed =ae39='s pair, and in round two
=ae39='s =clock_in= was no longer in the pending stream to clobber
=current=, so =a8a8='s id-less =clock_out= re-attributed to
=e51d6ba1= and produced =17:40--17:43=. Reproduced deterministically by
replaying the real =--review-items-from-queue= against a copy of the
queue with =ae39='s events marked applied.

That makes the defect *worse*, not milder, and reframes it: **heading
attribution of id-less events depends on watermark state.** The same
queue file yields different attribution depending on what was applied
before it — so the record a heading ends up with is a function of when
the human happened to run apply and how they batched it, not of what
the sessions actually did. A one-round apply loses the interval; a
two-round apply recovers it by luck. Neither is a property anyone
chose.

*The near miss is worse than the loss.* =ae39= paired correctly purely
because the agents completed LIFO. Reverse the finish order and
=ae39='s interval attributes to =e51d6ba1= — a real duration on the
wrong heading, which is the confidently-wrong record
[[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] exists to
prevent. Silent loss is the benign branch of this bug.

The irony: the *pairing* logic immediately below is fully agent-aware
(=by-agent=, config.el:2531-2554, "pair each agent's own
clock_in/clock_out"), and human spans correctly exclude agent events
(config.el:2562). Only the attribution walk that runs first is blind to
=agent_id=.

Likely fix: key =current= by =(session_id . agent_id)= rather than
=session_id= alone, with nil =agent_id= as the main session's own lane.
Cheap, and the failing case is now reproducible on demand — spawn two
concurrent subagents that each bracket their work with
=org_clock_in=/=org_clock_out=.

Note the nil bucket already held two other unattributed =clock_out=
events predating this experiment, and =b74e0f19= and =9d2fcdad= each
show a =clock_out= with no =clock_in=. Whether those share this root
cause or a different one is unexamined.

*Fixed 2026-08-13.* =--queue-events-by-id= now tracks the current
heading per *lane* rather than per session: =agent_id= when the event
came from a subagent, the main session otherwise. A =clock_out= with no
=id= resolves against its own lane, so interleaving cannot mis-pair it
regardless of completion order.

A second defect fell out of the same change, unplanned. Guideposts
carry no =agent_id= — the =Stop=/=UserPromptSubmit= hooks fire in the
parent's turn — so under the old code a subagent's =clock_in= captured
every following human =pause=/=resume=. Real evidence: =7771fc63='s
group contained a stray =[pause]= and produced a spurious zero-width
=:suggested:= span. Guideposts now stay in the main lane, where they
belong.

*Verified, not just implemented:*

- Two regression tests, both of which *fail* against pre-fix code
  (checked by reverting =config.el= and re-running, not assumed).
  =queue-attributes-concurrent-agents-separately= runs the measured
  interleaving in *both* completion orders — LIFO was what accidentally
  saved one agent, so testing only that order would have proved
  nothing. =queue-guideposts-ignore-subagent-lanes= pins the guidepost
  half. 193 -> 195 green.
- Replayed against the *real* captured queue at its pre-round
  watermark, in the live Emacs after =load-file=. That exact input
  previously yielded one interval; it now yields both, correctly
  attributed, *in a single pass* — so the two-round recovery this
  heading documented is no longer needed to get the right answer:

: e51d6ba1 start=17:40:01 end=17:43:58 agent=a8a8 suggested=nil
: 7771fc63 start=17:40:20 end=17:42:20 agent=ae39 suggested=nil

Not addressed here: the pre-existing unattributed =clock_out= events
noted above. They predate subagent activity, so they have a different
cause and are still unexamined.
*** TODO Background-task notifications write phantom resume guideposts :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       09c134c4-31eb-4f6c-aa10-f836d1d28f16
:CREATED:  [2026-08-12 Wed 17:46]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-12 Wed 17:47] \\
  phantom resume inflates human-present time
:END:

Observed 2026-08-12 during the subagent experiment behind
[[id:0d789b68-faca-4911-868a-fdd70acbfd40][0d789b68]]. A background
task completing re-invokes the model, which fires =UserPromptSubmit=,
which appends a =resume= guidepost — with no user having sent anything:

: 17:22:41  resume  UserPromptSubmit   (agent 1 finished ~17:21:47)
: 17:23:50  resume  UserPromptSubmit   (agent 2 finished ~17:23:15)

The events are byte-identical to a genuine prompt's; no field
distinguishes them. This matters because CLAUDE.md defines the
pause/resume stream as tracking *active work time only* — "paused the
moment Claude Code stops and is waiting on the user, resumed the moment
the user sends the next prompt." A robot wake-up now reads as the human
returning to the keyboard, and since
=--aggregate-guideposts= reconstructs human spans from exactly these
timestamps, the effect is to inflate apparent human-present time in
every =:suggested:= interval that spans a background completion.

Affects any background task, not just subagents. Note the guidepost
carries =agent_id: nil= even when the notification came from an agent —
the hook fires in the parent's turn, not the agent's — so filtering on
=agent_id= is *not* the fix.

Not yet investigated: whether the hook payload distinguishes a real
submission from a task-completion re-invocation at all. If it does not,
the honest options are to accept the inflation and document it, or to
stop treating =resume= as evidence of human presence.

*** DONE Apply's clock pair has no unwind-protect: a failed clock_out fabricates a huge interval :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       803314aa-eda9-4d48-9e43-b3eb4802a4e8
:CREATED:  [2026-08-12 Wed 17:46]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-08-13 Thu 09:43]--[2026-08-13 Thu 10:15] =>  0:32
CLOCK: [2026-08-12 Wed 19:00]--[2026-08-12 Wed 19:24] =>  0:24
- State "DONE"       from "TODO"       [2026-08-12 Wed 22:38] \\
  unwind-protect + cancel; 193 green
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-12 Wed 17:47] \\
  error path can strand an open clock line
- State "TODO"       from "NEXT"       [2026-08-12 Wed 22:39] \\
  Auto-demoted: superseded by sibling [[id:7387f97c-6c3e-4667-8851-203af024fdd9][Apply writes headless state notes -- RETRACTED, a grep artifact]] becoming NEXT.
- <2026-08-12 Wed 19:00>--<2026-08-12 Wed 19:24>
- <2026-08-13 Thu 09:43>--<2026-08-13 Thu 10:15>
:END:

Found 2026-08-12 while checking whether
[[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]]'s "apply writes
already-closed intervals" premise holds. It holds for a *crash* — no
=save-buffer= runs between the two calls — but not for an *error*.

=--review-apply-clock= (config.el:2741-2751) calls =org-clock-in= then
=org-clock-out= with no =unwind-protect= between them; the
=unwind-protect= at config.el:3457 guards the read-only flag only. If
=org-clock-out= signals after =org-clock-in= succeeded, =--at-id='s
=condition-case= (config.el:63) swallows it into an error string and
that item's own =save-buffer= is skipped — but the clock stays running
and the buffer stays modified. =--review-apply= then *continues the
loop* (config.el:2896), and the next item on the same file calls
=save-buffer= (config.el:2858), committing the open =CLOCK:= line to
disk. A stray =C-x C-s= by the user does the same.

*Corrected 2026-08-12 by forcing the failure and reading the result
off disk.* The mechanism above is right; the symptom stated was wrong,
and the real one is worse.

*No open =CLOCK:= line ever reaches disk.* What happens instead: item
A's =org-clock-out= fails, leaving the clock running; the *next* clock
item's =--review-apply-clock= hits its defensive =(when (org-clocking-p)
(org-clock-out nil t))= (config.el:2732), which closes A's clock at
*now*. Measured, with an intended 0:30 interval:

: CLOCK: [2026-08-12 Wed 10:00]--[2026-08-12 Wed 19:56] =>  9:56

The fabricated duration scales with the distance between the backdated
start and wall-clock time — and writing backdated intervals is the
entire purpose of apply, so in practice this inflates by hours, not
minutes.

Two aggravating details, both measured rather than predicted:

- Apply returns =(:applied 1 :errors ("Error: ..."))=. The human sees
  one clean failure and *no* signal that the failed heading gained an
  interval it never earned.
- The heading gets no annotation line at all, because
  =--append-to-drawer= (config.el:2752) sits after the clock block and
  is skipped by the same error. So the fabricated =CLOCK:= line looks
  exactly like a legitimate one, with nothing marking it suspect — the
  confidently-wrong record
  [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] exists to
  prevent.

Reproduction, described rather than linked because it lived in an
ephemeral session scratchpad: build a temp org file with two headings,
stub =org-clock-out= via =cl-letf= to signal on its *first* call only,
pass two clock items on the two headings to
=claude-code-ide-org--review-apply= in one batch, then read the file
back *from disk* rather than from the buffer — reading the buffer would
not have shown what was committed. Cheap to rebuild; a proper
regression test belongs in =config-test.el= as part of the fix.

*Fixed 2026-08-12.* An =unwind-protect= around the clock pair
(config.el:2741-2751); on a non-local exit it calls =org-clock-cancel=
rather than retrying the close. Cancelling is the honest cleanup: the
item is reported as an error, so its events are never marked applied
and the interval stays pending for a later pass. No record beats a
confident wrong one, and retrying a call that just signalled is not a
recovery strategy. The cancel is wrapped in =ignore-errors= so a
failure there cannot mask the original error, which is the one the
human needs to see.

*Verified, not just implemented:* a new regression test
(=review-failed-clock-out-writes-no-interval=) stubs =org-clock-out= to
signal on its first call, applies two clock items in one batch, and
asserts *exactly one* =CLOCK:= line reaches disk — the surviving item's,
at its exact endpoints. It *fails* against pre-fix code and passes
after, which is the property the three tests written for
[[id:7387f97c-6c3e-4667-8851-203af024fdd9][7387f97c]] conspicuously
lacked. 192 -> 193 green. Re-running the original live-Emacs probe after
=load-file= confirms the same end to end: the failed heading now carries
no drawer at all, and =org-clock-out= is called twice rather than three
times, because the next item's defensive close no longer finds a clock
running.

*Still unestablished, and it gates how urgent this was rather than
whether the fix is right:* whether =org-clock-out=
can actually signal here with both endpoints supplied and
=org-clock-out-remove-zero-time-clocks= bound nil. The probe simulates
the failure rather than provoking a real one, so this demonstrates the
*consequence* of a premise that has not itself been shown reachable.
Reading =org-clock.el= for reachable signal paths is the remaining
step. If none exists, this is a latent trap rather than a live bug —
worth fixing cheaply either way, since an =unwind-protect= around the
pair costs one line.

Originally reached by two separate reviews of the apply path, both
reasoning from the same code. That is no longer offered as
corroboration: two readers making the same inference from one text is
not independent confirmation, as [[id:7387f97c-6c3e-4667-8851-203af024fdd9][7387f97c]]
demonstrated the same day.

*Premise confirmed reachable, 2026-08-13 — this was a live bug, not
insurance.* The open question was whether =org-clock-out= can actually
signal on apply's path, with both endpoints supplied and
=org-clock-out-remove-zero-time-clocks= bound nil. Read =org-clock.el=:
it has exactly two signal sites, both bypassed when =fail-quietly= is
non-nil and apply passes nil:

- =(user-error "No active clock")= — guarded, since apply checks
  =org-clocking-p= immediately after its =org-clock-in=.
- =(error "Clock start time is gone")= (org-clock.el:1841) — raised when
  the line at =org-clock-marker= no longer looks like a =CLOCK:= line.

The second is *reachable, and observed*. It is the identical error
recorded as incident 1 of
[[id:53b0047d-c55c-486c-8eed-ba4994d97a1a][53b0047d]], whose subject is
precisely a desynced =org-clock-marker=. So the chain is: a direct file
write desyncs the marker -> apply's =org-clock-out= signals -> without
the =unwind-protect= the clock stays open -> the next clock item's
defensive close writes a fabricated interval at *now*.

Consequence for sequencing: =53b0047d= is upstream of this heading and
should not be cancelled while this failure mode matters. Recorded there
too.

One further path worth noting, not fixed here:
=claude-code-ide-org--audit-clock-hook= runs on =org-clock-out-hook=
*unguarded*, and org runs that hook only after the =CLOCK:= line is
written (org-clock.el:~1898). A signal from it would leave a correct
interval plus a failed item — a different and milder shape than the one
fixed, but it would still mark the item failed while its write stood.
*** CANCELLED Apply writes headless state notes -- RETRACTED, a grep artifact :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       7387f97c-6c3e-4667-8851-203af024fdd9
:CREATED:  [2026-08-12 Wed 19:14]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-08-12 Wed 22:36]--[2026-08-12 Wed 22:41] =>  0:05
CLOCK: [2026-08-12 Wed 19:46]--[2026-08-12 Wed 19:58] =>  0:12
- State "CANCELLED"  from "DOING"      [2026-08-12 Wed 19:45] \\
  no defect; grep artifact, see body
CLOCK: [2026-08-12 Wed 19:33]--[2026-08-12 Wed 19:42] =>  0:09
- State "DOING"      from "NEXT"       [2026-08-12 Wed 19:25] \\
  diagnosing the missing State header
- State "NEXT"       from              [2026-08-12 Wed 19:15] \\
  native state logging silently malformed
- <2026-08-12 Wed 19:33>--<2026-08-12 Wed 19:42> fixing headless state notes
- <2026-08-12 Wed 19:46>--<2026-08-12 Wed 19:58>
- <2026-08-12 Wed 22:36>--<2026-08-12 Wed 22:41>
:END:

*Retracted 2026-08-12 the same day it was filed. There was no defect.*
Every transition the apply pass wrote carries org's full native header,
correctly formed:

: - State "CANCELLED"  from "MAYBE"      [2026-08-12 Wed 17:37] \\
:   :SESSIONS: deleted; ordering settled by af7d3687

All eleven applied transitions have one; =grep -c '^- State "'= counts
55 in the file.

*The defect was an artifact of the command used to look for it.* The
diff was filtered through =grep -v "^[+-][+-]"=, intended to drop the
=+++=/=---= file headers. That pattern also drops every *added* line
beginning with =-= — which is exactly the shape of an org log entry
(=+- State "CANCELLED" ...=). What survived the filter was the indented
continuation lines alone, which read convincingly as headless notes.

Kept rather than deleted because the investigation it triggered
produced things worth having, and because the failure mode is worth a
name:

- Three tests survive in =config-test.el= pinning apply's state-log
  format — with a note, without one, and with the trigger hooks firing.
  They assert correct behavior that nothing asserted before, which is a
  real gap this closed even though the bug was imaginary.
- Two probes established that apply writes correct headers in the
  *live* Doom session under every condition tried: read-only buffer
  cleared mid-flight by =--review-ensure-writable=, and a four-item
  batch containing a three-link chain. Both were written to test
  hypotheses about a defect that did not exist; both are reusable.
- CLAUDE.md's documented =#+TODO:= line is genuinely wrong — it omits
  the =(t!)= log markers the real files have carried since =a943f61=.
  Found while chasing this. Belongs to
  [[id:02aaae22-0958-4c0f-b5bb-2cbea32416ae][02aaae22]].

*The lesson, stated plainly because it recurred three times in one
sitting:* every wrong conclusion here came from trusting a derived view
— a filtered diff, a fixed-width =sed= window that crossed heading
boundaries, a scratch file built from documentation instead of the real
header — over the artifact itself. The file said the opposite each
time, and reading it directly took one command. Prefer the primary
artifact; treat any filter as a hypothesis about what it hides.
*** WAIT Let a session interject without waiting: advisory locks, or extend the queue to capture/amend :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       b5f94b88-76e3-4a3c-ac73-729f34d33691
:CREATED:  [2026-08-13 Thu 10:13]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "WAIT"       from "DOING"      [2026-08-13 Thu 12:19] \\
  awaiting go-ahead to start implementing
CLOCK: [2026-08-13 Thu 11:25]--[2026-08-13 Thu 11:30] =>  0:05
- State "DOING"      from "PLANNING"   [2026-08-13 Thu 11:23]
- State "PLANNING"   from "TODO"       [2026-08-13 Thu 11:15] \\
  planning the capture/amend queue extension
CLOCK: [2026-08-13 Thu 11:04]--[2026-08-13 Thu 11:14] =>  0:10
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-13 Thu 10:14] \\
  drafted, awaiting the user's careful read
- <2026-08-13 Thu 11:04>--<2026-08-13 Thu 11:14>
- <2026-08-13 Thu 11:25>--<2026-08-13 Thu 11:30> planning capture/amend queue extension
- <2026-08-13 Thu 12:18>--<2026-08-13 Thu 12:18> planning capture/amend queue extension
:END:

[[file:~/.claude/plans/capture-amend-queue.md][Plan]]

*Plan approved 2026-08-13, scoped by two decisions the user made during
planning:* v1 covers *capture and amend* only — the structural writers
(=org_refile=, =org_archive=, =org_move_sibling=, =org_sort_children=)
stay immediate — and capture *writes through when the file is free*,
queueing only when it is busy, so today's immediacy survives and only
contended writes defer. That is the user's original lock idea made
non-blocking, and it supersedes the "always queue" reading of step 3
below.

Note the plan lives in its own file rather than this conversation's
=hazy-moseying-sky.md=: that path is already the permanent link for
[[id:6b1e73c4-25da-4f0e-8a51-9c0d3f7ab214][6b1e73c4]] and
[[id:f290161c-f9e7-4bfb-9adf-4c5630d4c5ae][f290161c]], and Claude Code
reuses one plan file per conversation, so planning here overwrote it.
Restored from =plans/= and the new plan given its own name, so both
older links still resolve to the plan they were written for.

*Read by the user 2026-08-13* — both the original draft below and the
=create-lockfiles= research appended to step 1. The "study this before
signing off" reminder it was captured under is discharged.

*No verdict was recorded, and none should be inferred.* The user said
only that they had reviewed it and that the heading could be amended
accordingly; they did not approve the recommendation, reject it, or
choose between its three steps. So this remains a proposal Claude
drafted, not an agreed design, and the three load-bearing claims are
still open to challenge: that a *blocking* lock is the wrong primitive,
that the underlying need is a missing queue rather than a missing lock,
and that the ordering below is the right one. Anyone acting on this
should get an explicit decision first.

*The ask, in the user's words:* so many times they want to ask a
question or make a proposal while something else is in flight, which
often leads to something new to capture or amend. The literal proposal
was a file or Emacs buffer lock/semaphore on =TODO.org= and friends,
causing concurrent sessions to wait their turn while another holds it.

*What actually collides today.*

The queue already removed most of the contention: =todo= and clock
events are append-only, per-session, written blind with =O_APPEND=, so
two sessions cannot corrupt each other there by construction. What
remains is *direct writes to the file*:

- =org_capture=, =org_refile=, =org_archive=, =org_move_sibling= and
  =org_sort_children=, which all still =save-buffer= immediately
- the org skill's prose edits via Claude Code's =Edit= tool, which
  bypass Emacs entirely and write to disk
- the user's own hand-edits in the live buffer
- =.git/index=, a separate incident already owned by
  [[id:2aeb65d6-350e-4c75-811e-4a748044fe2c][2aeb65d6]]

Not hypothetical: on 2026-08-13 two edits in one session hit "the file
had been modified on disk since you last read it". The ad-hoc
mitigation in use — checking =buffer-modified-p= before touching the
file — is a hand-rolled version of what this heading proposes.

*Why a blocking lock looks like the wrong primitive.*

Recorded as the argument against, because "why not just take a lock" is
the obvious question a future reader will ask.

1. *Waiting reintroduces the failure this architecture exists to avoid.*
   A session blocked on a lock burns wall-clock with no way to report
   why, and waits forever if the holder crashed. Same shape as the
   non-interactive =emacsclient= hang that pushed this project to
   append-only.
2. *The contention is asymmetric.* The human is the authoritative
   writer; Claude sessions should yield to them and never the reverse. A
   symmetric semaphore models the wrong relationship.
3. *It cannot be enforced where it matters most.* The =Edit= tool
   consults neither Emacs nor any lockfile, so any lock is advisory for
   exactly the writer most likely to be mid-flight. A mechanism that
   looks like mutual exclusion but is not is worse than an honest
   advisory one.
4. *Stale locks are stale clocks again.* An owner, a TTL and a recovery
   path — the subsystem
   [[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]] is currently
   deciding whether to retire, rebuilt for a new resource.

*The reframe this rests on — the part most worth challenging.*

The ask may not be mutual exclusion at all. Note where the user's own
sentence ends: the interruption *leads to something new to capture or
amend*. That is a missing queue, not a missing lock — state changes
already work that way; capture and amend do not.

If that reframe is wrong — if the real need is genuinely "stop the
other writer" rather than "do not lose my thought" — then the
recommendation below is wrong too, and a fail-fast lock alone is the
answer.

*Proposed, cheapest first.*

1. *Turn on =create-lockfiles=.* Measured 2026-08-13: it is =nil=, Doom's
   default, so Emacs's own mechanism is currently off entirely. One
   line. It will not stop the =Edit= tool, but it makes Emacs-mediated
   collisions visible rather than silent. Checked before proposing
   anything bespoke, per
   [[id:c084553c-0621-4a96-9fa1-f32850aeec6a][c084553c]]'s standing
   instruction.

   *Why Doom disables it, checked 2026-08-13 at the user's request.*
   =lisp/doom-emacs.el:462= sets it under a =Backup & autosave files=
   heading, sharing one comment with =make-backup-files=: "Don't
   generate backups or lockfiles. ... This is better suited to version
   control, and I don't want world-readable copies of potentially
   sensitive material floating around our filesystem." Older Doom
   (=core/core-editor.el=) worded it the same way.

   That rationale is about *backups*, and does not actually describe a
   lock file. Per the Emacs Lisp manual, a lock file is a symlink named
   =.#FILE= whose *target* is the string =user@host.pid:boot=; it holds
   no contents of the edited file at all. So the "copies of sensitive
   material" argument does not apply to the thing it is being used to
   justify — lockfiles were bundled in with backups under one heading
   and inherited a comment written for backups. A residual privacy point
   survives (the target leaks username, host and PID), but that is a
   much smaller claim than the one made.

   The real-world objection in the wider community is *tooling*, not
   privacy: =.#FILE= is a dangling symlink, and file watchers and build
   tools choke on it (e.g. =ember-cli= issue #6527, "=(setq
   create-lockfiles nil)= shouldn't be necessary for Emacs usage"). That
   risk is low for this repo — nothing here watches the tree — but it is
   the reason to check before flipping it globally rather than
   per-directory.

   *And one point actively in favor, which is why this step is listed at
   all:* on detecting a lock, Emacs calls =ask-user-about-lock=, which
   offers grab / ignore / abort. It *asks*; it does not wait. That is
   precisely the fail-fast shape argued for in step 2, already
   implemented upstream.
2. *A fail-fast advisory lock in the MCP write tools* — a refusal, not a
   wait: "another session is writing TODO.org; queued instead." Fast
   failure is debuggable and cannot deadlock.
3. *Extend the queue to capture/amend proposals.* The interjection
   becomes an event, nothing waits, and the existing human review pass
   applies it — which also makes it reviewable rather than immediate,
   the property already chosen deliberately for state changes.

Steps 2 and 3 overlap: if 3 lands, 2 may be unnecessary, since there
would be nothing left that needs to write immediately.
*** TODO Re-measure the guidepost gap threshold against a month of queue data :code:research:
SCHEDULED: <2026-09-13 Sun +1m>
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       96a51c2f-ce11-4b40-bdff-11223d3d4eb7
:CREATED:  [2026-08-13 Thu 12:20]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-13 Thu 12:21] \\
  scheduled re-measure, one month out
:END:

Scheduled rather than left to memory: the finding below is real but
thin, and the failure mode is adopting a four-day number as permanent
because nobody re-ran it.

*The survey, 2026-08-13.* 422 queue events, 2026-08-10 to 08-13, across
six sessions. Measured the *queue* rather than =:LOGBOOK:= deliberately:
=:LOGBOOK:= intervals are reconstructions produced by the very threshold
under test, so measuring them would partly measure the parameter.

Decomposed each session's stream into the two gaps that mean different
things:

- =resume= -> =pause= — how long the agent worked. n=111, median *81s*,
  p90 *211s*, one outlier over an hour (the concurrent subagent run).
- =pause= -> =resume= — how long the human took to reply. n=150, median
  *181s*.

*The result, and it is sharper than expected: the second is bimodal with
an empty band between the modes.* Sorted gaps run =… 949, 971, 1061=
and then jump straight to =2070, 2103, 2135 …=. **No gap anywhere in the
corpus falls between 1061s and 2070s.** A threshold sweep sees the same
hole from the other side: span count falls steeply, then sits flat at 29
across 1200-1800s, then resumes falling. Every value inside the band
produces an identical reconstruction.

Acted on immediately: =claude-code-ide-org-guidepost-gap-threshold= 900
-> *1200s*, the round number inside the band, at the start of the
plateau with margin either side. 900 sat just below the band and split
five spans nothing justifies splitting.

*A stated assumption the data contradicted, which is the more useful
half.* The survey was proposed on the premise that silence from ~18:00
to ~08:00 is almost certainly AFK. True, but not where the long gaps
are: of 19 gaps over 2000s, *11 start inside working hours* — 07:00,
10:00, 12:00, 14:00, 15:00, 16:00, 17:00 — including the two longest
(151 and 158 minutes, starting 12:59 and 15:34). Daytime absence is
indistinguishable from nighttime absence. Gap *length* separates the
modes cleanly; clock hour does not. Recorded against
[[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]], whose
working-hours guess rests on exactly the premise this refutes.

*What to re-run on the scheduled date*, and the reason the number is not
yet settled: n=150 gaps, four days, one person, during an unusually
intense stretch of work on this very project. The band may be an artifact
of that. Re-derive the histogram and the sweep; if the band has moved or
closed, retune again and say so here rather than defending 1200.

*This is a moving target, not a one-off calibration* — the user's own
framing, 2026-08-13, and it changes what the re-measure is for.
Proficiency, setup and work objectives all evolve, and the habits
generating these gaps evolve with them. A faster operator, a different
mix of long-running subagent work, or a stretch of shallow triage rather
than deep implementation would each shift the modes and could close the
band entirely. So the finding above describes *August 2026*, not this
project. The scheduled task is not "confirm 1200 was right"; it is
"re-derive, and expect a different answer."

*Made a =+1m= repeater at the user's instruction, 2026-08-13*, which is
the honest encoding of "moving target": a one-shot date would have
implied the number gets settled once. It does not. Each occurrence
re-derives the histogram and the sweep against whatever the preceding
month's habits actually were, and either confirms the current value or
moves it.

Practical note for whoever handles an occurrence: marking it =DONE=
rolls the =SCHEDULED= stamp forward a month and returns the heading to
=TODO= — org's own repeater behavior — so the record of *that* month's
finding has to be written into this body before closing it, or it is
gone. Append; do not overwrite the previous month's numbers.

Charts from the original survey are an artifact, not committed here —
regenerate rather than trusting a stale rendering.** TODO Institute a catch-all heading for cross-cutting meta-work :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       a1d63f52-4c07-4b9e-8f31-6d2b90c4e7a5
:CREATED:  [2026-08-10 Mon 12:50]
:END:

Proposed by the user 2026-08-10: a standing pseudo-task to clock
against for grooming, capturing, "what's next" review, and similar
activity that is real work but belongs to no single tracked task.
Captured as TODO on a recommendation, not as a settled decision.

*The concrete argument for it, from this session's own data.* Of the
first ten events in this session's queue, *eight* were =pause=/=resume=
guideposts that reduced to nothing at review time. Guideposts carry no
heading; they inherit whichever one the surrounding
=clock_in=/=clock_out= last named, so guideposts recorded while no clock
is open are unattributable and silently dropped. Meta-work is precisely
when no clock is open. So today that time is not merely misattributed --
it is invisible, which is in direct tension with this project's second
stated goal of trustworthy tracking of where attention actually went.

A standing task, clocked during that work, gives those guideposts a
bracket to attach to. Nothing else has to change for it to work.

*Check org's own mechanism first, per
[[id:c084553c-0621-4a96-9fa1-f32850aeec6a][c084553c]].* Org already has
a name for this concept: =org-clock-default-task=, set with
=org-clock-mark-default-task=, a designated fallback task you can clock
into directly. This should almost certainly use that rather than invent
a parallel convention -- exactly the reflex c084553c exists to enforce.

*Two real risks, neither disqualifying:*
- *The dumping-ground failure.* If ambiguous time drifts here by
  default, the record gets *less* informative, not more -- a large
  bucket labelled "misc" answers no question worth asking. Scope it to
  genuinely cross-cutting work, and treat the review pass as the place
  to re-attribute a span that turned out to be about one task.
- *It never reaches DONE.* A permanent open task sits in a sibling group
  alongside the single-NEXT-per-level triggers
  (=--trigger-auto-promote-sole-todo=,
  =--trigger-demote-conflicting-next=). Check how those behave with a
  sibling that is deliberately never finished before relying on it. It
  also never archives, so its =:LOGBOOK:= grows without bound -- probably
  fine for a pure time record, but it is a decision, not an oversight.

Independent of [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] and
useful either way: attribution is resolved from queued
=clock_in=/=clock_out= events both before and after the cutover, so the
bracket does the same job in both worlds.


*Refined 2026-08-10, and it narrows what this heading is for.* The user
proposed the general principle: grooming, defining, amending and
adjusting a future task is *activity on that task*, and should be clocked
against it — leaving its TODO state untouched. Recorded here because it
answers most of what a catch-all was being considered for.

*This was never stated anywhere, though nothing forbade it.* CLAUDE.md's
transition rules say a transition *to* =DOING= opens a clock and one
*from* =DOING= closes it. They never say the converse — that a running
clock implies =DOING= — so clocking a =TODO= heading has always been
legal by the letter and simply undescribed. Confirmed safe in practice
the same day: =org-clock-in-switch-to-state= is nil here, so clocking in
does not touch the keyword, and this heading's own editorial session was
clocked against =02aaae22= while it stayed literally =TODO=.

*Why it is better than a bucket, where it applies.* Attribution is exact:
time spent working out what a task should be lands on that task, next to
the reasoning it produced, and shows up in any report scoped to it.
A catch-all would answer "how much meta-work happened" but never "which
task was I circling."

*So the two are complementary, and this heading shrinks to the residue:*
work that genuinely belongs to no single task — "what's next?" surveys
across the whole backlog, cross-cutting review, triage before a task
exists to attach to. That residue is real but much smaller than the
original framing assumed, which also blunts the dumping-ground risk noted
above: with per-task grooming as the default, anything reaching the
catch-all is there because it truly has no home.

*Consequence worth accepting deliberately:* a =TODO= heading can now
accrue real clock time without ever having been =DOING=. That is correct
— deciding what a task is *is* work — but it means "time spent" and
"was worked on" stop being the same question, and any future reporting
needs to keep them apart.

*Needs a rule in CLAUDE.md* under "State transition rules", stating that
a clock may run on a heading in any state, and that grooming attaches to
the task being groomed. Distinct from
[[id:02aaae22-0958-4c0f-b5bb-2cbea32416ae][02aaae22]], which is scoped to
documenting the post-cutover architecture; this is a general convention
that holds regardless of the refactor's fate.


*Alternative captured 2026-08-11, and it narrows this heading further:*
[[id:3d0487f4-b8f8-451c-88bf-ab537663cbd0][3d0487f4]] proposes that the review buffer
surface unattributed guideposts and let the human assign them to a
heading retrospectively, rather than requiring anyone to clock into a
standing task in advance. If that lands, this heading is no longer a
mechanism to remember — it is just a convenient assignment target for
spans that genuinely belong to no task.
*** TODO An assigned span writes a label-less annotation line :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       c97b3564-5b14-4fc5-8964-a64775c5dc5d
:CREATED:  [2026-08-13 Thu 13:50]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-13 Thu 13:51] \\
  label-less annotation from assigned spans
:END:

Found 2026-08-13 in the first real apply of
[[id:3d0487f4-b8f8-451c-88bf-ab537663cbd0][3d0487f4]]'s spans. Compare
what the two span paths wrote into the same drawer:

: - <2026-08-13 Thu 12:24>--<2026-08-13 Thu 13:09>
: - <2026-08-13 Thu 13:16>--<2026-08-13 Thu 13:22> building unassigned span assignment

The first is an *assigned* span, the second an ordinary bracketed one.
Unassigned spans are built with =:note nil= because there is no
enclosing =clock_in= to inherit a label from — correct as far as it
goes, but the resulting annotation restates the =CLOCK:= line directly
above it and adds nothing. =clock-template.org='s convention is that
every non-=CLOCK:= line carries a short label; a bare one is noise
against that standard.

Two candidate fixes, and the choice is a real one:
1. *Suppress the annotation entirely* when there is no note. Cheapest,
   and the =CLOCK:= line already carries the interval. Cost: the
   *active* timestamps disappear with it, and those are deliberate —
   they are what puts a human span into =org-agenda= (recorded on
   =--review-format-annotation=). Suppressing would silently remove a
   span from the agenda, which is a behaviour change disguised as
   tidying.
2. *Synthesise a label* — "assigned at review", or the heading the human
   picked, or the fact that it was a suggestion accepted rather than
   chosen. Keeps the agenda timestamps and says something true about
   provenance, which the bracketed path cannot say.

Leaning (2) on the agenda point, but it is worth checking whether a
label-less annotation actually reaches =org-agenda= before deciding —
if it does not, the argument for (1) is much stronger.

*Shipped 2026-08-13* (commit =8e9a678=), taking option 2 — synthesise a
label. The check that decided it: a label-less annotation still reaches
=org-agenda=, because the agenda matches the *timestamp*, not the text
around it. So suppressing the line would have quietly dropped the span
from the agenda — a behaviour change wearing the clothes of a tidy-up,
and the cheaper-looking option was the wrong one.

The synthesised label carries *provenance*, the one thing the timestamps
cannot say: this interval was reconstructed and confirmed, not clocked
as it happened. Two variants, because accepting a suggestion and
choosing a heading are different acts — =suggested span, accepted at
review= and =assigned at review=. A real note always wins over both.

Implementation note worth keeping: this needed a *permanent* =:origin=
tag alongside the transient =:unassigned=, which =a= clears. Without it
the annotation could not tell, after assignment, that the interval had
been reconstructed at all.

*Verified live, not just tested.* Before: 17 bare =- <ts>--<ts>= lines
in a single apply pass. After: every span written carries its label, and
the first real one read
=- <2026-08-10 Mon 12:36>--<2026-08-10 Mon 12:59> assigned at review=.
Fixed going forward only, at the user's instruction; the 17 already
written stand as they are.

*** TODO Reconsider consolidating at apply: the rounding hazard that forbade it is retired :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       12e0adac-5aa4-4e95-a5d5-574ce32c1db0
:CREATED:  [2026-08-13 Thu 13:50]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-13 Thu 13:51] \\
  stale rationale; consolidation may be safe now
:END:

*A documented rationale has outlived its cause.* Apply deliberately does
not consolidate the drawer it writes into, and
=--aggregate-guideposts='s docstring gives the reason:

: Nothing here rounds: `claude-code-ide-org--consolidate-logbook-text's
: 5-minute rounding silently drops any interval under ~2.5 minutes that
: misses a boundary, which is precisely the confidently-wrong record
: this design exists to prevent.

That was true when written and is false now.
=--consolidate-logbook-text='s own docstring opens *"This function no
longer alters any interval"* — the rounding was retired outright in
[[id:b74e0f19-5a26-4c83-9d70-8e1c5a2f6b04][b74e0f19]], the incident
where it erased a real two-minute interval. So the hazard that kept
consolidation off the apply path no longer exists, and the comment
asserting it is stale in a way that would survive review indefinitely,
because it reads as a settled decision rather than a live claim.

*What this makes possible.* After the 2026-08-13 apply,
[[id:c954f650-b92f-4920-9967-ab6c44577b30][c954f650]]'s drawer reads:

: CLOCK: [2026-08-13 Thu 12:24]--[2026-08-13 Thu 13:09] =>  0:45
: - State "TODO"       from "MAYBE"      [2026-08-13 Thu 12:21] \\
:   ungated; gate was unsatisfiable and a proxy
: CLOCK: [2026-08-10 Mon 20:48]--[2026-08-10 Mon 20:49] =>  0:01
: - <2026-08-13 Thu 12:24>--<2026-08-13 Thu 13:09>

Four entries, four different positions on the timeline, in none of them.
[[id:af7d3687-6d1e-4480-a9e2-df772785713f][af7d3687]] built exactly the
function that fixes this and proved it a byte-identical fixed point
against =clock-template.org=, but nothing calls it: it came off the
clock-out path and never gained another caller. Its own accepted cost
was that "a live drawer drifts back out of order between
consolidations" — and this is that drift, now visible in real data
rather than predicted.

*Why this is a decision and not a fix.* Consolidating at apply changes
what apply writes, and apply's whole claim is that it writes exactly
what the human confirmed and nothing else. Reordering lines the human
did not ask to reorder is a small violation of that, even if the result
is more legible. Points to weigh:
- It is *idempotent* and provably lossless — =af7d3687= pins both, and a
  second pass is asserted to be a no-op.
- It touches lines the current apply never wrote, including history from
  other sessions, so a bug in it damages more than the batch.
- The alternative is a separate human-invoked command, which keeps apply
  minimal at the cost of another thing to remember.

Whatever is decided, the stale comment on =--aggregate-guideposts= must
be corrected either way — it currently misinforms anyone reasoning about
this.
*** NEXT Editing a span's interval does not re-scope its events, so truncating loses the remainder :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       ffe65444-50ed-49f8-a4c3-27c1c4c843c3
:CREATED:  [2026-08-13 Thu 15:42]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "NEXT"       from              [2026-08-13 Thu 15:42] \\
  silent loss on truncate; recovery is available
:END:

*Found 2026-08-13 by losing 32 minutes of record to it*, acting on
Claude's own advice. Recorded with that provenance because the advice
was the proximate cause, not the code alone.

A 54-minute unassigned span (08-10 18:12--19:07) straddled two tasks.
The suggested remedy was to truncate it with =e= to the first task's
portion and let the remainder return on a later pass. Truncation worked
— =CLOCK: [18:12]--[18:35] =>  0:23= landed correctly — but the
remainder did not return. The guideposts at 18:32, 18:50, 18:51 and
19:07 are all marked *applied*.

*Mechanism.* =claude-code-ide-org-review-edit-interval= rewrites
=:start= and =:end= and deliberately clears =:suggested=, but never
touches =:events=. =--review-apply= then marks every event in that list
consumed via =--review-record-applied=. So an edited item writes the
*narrowed* interval while consuming the *original* span's events. The
narrower the edit, the more is silently swallowed.

*The claim that was wrong, stated plainly so it is not repeated:* "the
events you don't apply stay queued, so the tail isn't lost." That holds
for an item you *skip* — =--review-record-applied='s docstring says so
explicitly, and it is what makes applying a subset safe — but not for an
item you *edit and apply*. Skipping and narrowing are different
operations and only one of them is conservative.

*Nothing is destroyed*, which is the only reason this is a defect and
not an incident: the =.jsonl= is append-only and every event is still
there. Only the watermark asserts they are consumed. Recovery is
removing those timestamps from the =.applied= set, after which the
remainder is re-offered as its own span — and content idempotency
(=78f485a8=) makes re-offering safe, since the 18:12--18:35 interval
already on disk cannot be written twice.

*Candidate fixes, in rough order of appeal:*
1. *Re-scope =:events= when the interval is edited* — keep only those
   falling inside the new endpoints. Makes truncation mean what everyone
   assumed it meant, and the remainder genuinely returns. The obvious
   fix, and it makes =e= a real splitting tool.
2. *Refuse to narrow*, allowing only widening. Cheap and safe, but
   removes the one workaround for a span covering two tasks.
3. *Warn* that N events fall outside the new interval and will be
   consumed anyway. Honest, but leaves the sharp edge in place.

(1) also nearly delivers what
[[id:3d0487f4-b8f8-451c-88bf-ab537663cbd0][3d0487f4]] listed as
explicitly out of scope — splitting one span across two headings —
since truncate-assign-repeat becomes a working split once the remainder
survives.

*Shipped 2026-08-13* (commit =be5834d=), taking option 1 and adding the
user's refinement: the remainder appears *on the spot*, in the same
buffer, rather than at the next refresh.

=review-edit-interval= now partitions =:events= at the new endpoints,
keeps the ones the interval still spans, and hands the rest to
=--review-insert-remainders=, which clusters them at the usual gap
threshold and appends them as fresh items. A remainder inherits the
heading as a *suggestion* but reverts to unassigned when the original
was reconstructed — narrowing usually means the tail belongs elsewhere,
which is the whole reason the human reached for =e=. It never inherits
=:marked=: a remainder is a new decision, not part of the batch already
confirmed.

*Why on the spot matters*, and it was the user's point: the remainder is
the entire reason to narrow, so the human is mid-thought about which
task the tail belongs to. Deferring it to the next refresh is where it
gets abandoned.

*The lost 32 minutes were recovered first,* and the recovery is the best
evidence for the design this heading sits inside: the =.jsonl= is
append-only, so un-marking three timestamps in the =.applied= sidecar
brought the interval back as its own span (18:50--19:07), which was then
assigned and applied normally. Nothing had to be reconstructed by hand.

*Verified:* 211 tests, the new one confirmed to fail against the unfixed
code. It asserts the conservation property directly — every event of the
original span is still accounted for, in either the narrowed item or the
remainder.

*Consequence beyond the fix:* =e= is now a working span splitter —
truncate, assign, repeat — which
[[id:3d0487f4-b8f8-451c-88bf-ab537663cbd0][3d0487f4]] had listed as
explicitly out of scope for v1.
** MAYBE Add a third retention tier for completed work as a chronological changelog :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       4c0044bf-1b6c-44ea-807a-8f9e3523e3fd
:CREATED:  [2026-08-07 Fri 09:41]
:END:

Captured 2026-08-07 from conversation. Proposal: TODO.org (live) →
DONE.org (archive) → a terse, permanent, conventional-timeline changelog,
so DONE.org can be pruned or semantically compressed as work recedes
without losing the record entirely.

*Measured first, because the diagnosis moves:* DONE.org holds 22 entries
and 955 lines, every one archived on =2026-07-29= or =-07-30= — archiving
has not run since. Meanwhile TODO.org is 2444 lines and still carries
*13* =DONE=/=CANCELLED= headings, the oldest at line 7. The pressure is
not tier 2 overflowing; it is tier 1→2 having stalled, so completed work
accumulates in the live file. A third tier added now would relieve none
of that. Restore the archiving cadence first and re-measure — tier 3 may
not be needed for a long time.

*If built anyway, three constraints:*
- *Referential integrity is the real risk.* 21 distinct =[[id:...]]=
  links point into archived headings today, from other org files,
  CLAUDE.md, and the auto-memory folder — two of them added
  2026-08-07 citing =6a6d5b4e=. A conventional Markdown changelog has no
  stable per-entry identifier, so pruning breaks every inbound link
  silently. Any tier-3 format must carry the original =:ID:=, or leave an
  ID-bearing stub behind in DONE.org.
- *Git is already a chronological changelog*, so the question is what
  this adds. The honest answer: git records what *changed*; DONE.org
  records what was *learned* and why the obvious approach failed. That is
  the part with no other home — and it means tier 3's content should be
  biased toward decisions and dead ends, not shipped features, which git
  already covers.
- *Compression is where this project's signature failure mode lives.*
  The most valuable sentence in =6a6d5b4e= is that the initial hypothesis
  was a red herring — precisely what terse summarization drops first,
  because it does not look like an outcome. Editorial rule: keep what
  would change a future decision, drop what is re-derivable from the
  code. Model-driven compression without human review is the same
  confidently-wrong pattern [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]]
  exists to eliminate; make it diffable and reviewed.

*Format: stay in org, don't leave for Markdown.* =org-archive.el= already
supports =datetree/= in =org-archive-location= (org-archive.el:284), so
=#+ARCHIVE: DONE.org::datetree/* Done= buys chronological structure with
no new code, while preserving =[[id:]]= resolution, =org-ql=
queryability, and tags. A =CHANGELOG.md= discards all of that for the
keepachangelog convention, which is release-oriented — and this project
has no releases. Checked per
[[id:c084553c-0621-4a96-9fa1-f32850aeec6a][c084553c]]'s standing
instruction to exhaust org's own machinery first.

Open: what triggers a prune (size, age, or deliberate act — probably
deliberate); whether tier 3 is its own =CHANGELOG.org= or a datetree
inside DONE.org; and whether anything should enumerate inbound =[[id:]]=
references before a prune, since nothing does today.

* Observability

Broader than clocking — visibility into what every tool call
actually did, both after the fact and at the start of a session.

** TODO Make task lifecycle answerable from one timeline, starting at creation :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       d9132c57-9dcf-47e9-ba74-7f5a1ced3ae5
:CREATED:  [2026-08-11 Tue 10:32]
:END:

Prompted 2026-08-11 by a concrete reporting question — "how long did it
take from capture time to the PLANNING phase, for each of these tasks?"
— which is the first properly articulated demand on this project's
second stated goal, trustworthy tracking of where attention actually
went. It cannot be answered today, and the reason is not the one it
looks like.

*Measured 2026-08-11:*

: TODO.org  headings carrying :CREATED:   56
: TODO.org  "- State ..." lines            7
: DONE.org  "- State ..." lines            0

All seven come from the two hooks that write their note explicitly
(=--trigger-auto-promote-sole-todo=, =--promote-planning-to-doing=). Not
one came from =org_set_todo=, which binds =org-inhibit-logging= to =t=
unconditionally for every state. So the transition history barely
exists, and in DONE.org — where post-hoc analysis would actually look —
it does not exist at all. That zero is *not* the archiver stripping
drawers: =org-archive-subtree= moves the whole subtree intact. Nothing
was ever written.

The example question is the worst case of this. =PLANNING= is transient
— auto-promoted to =DOING= the instant a plan is approved — and the
same census found *zero* headings sitting in it. Current state can
therefore never answer "when did this reach PLANNING"; only a
transition log can.

*So the blocker is the missing log, not =:CREATED:='s location,* and
[[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] already fixes it:
apply runs inside a genuinely interactive command, which is what lets
org's native logging fire instead of being suppressed. This heading is
the remaining half — making the timeline start at the right place — and
is close to worthless before the cutover, since there would be a
creation line and then silence.

*Proposal: duplicate =:CREATED:= into =:LOGBOOK:= as a creation entry.
Do not move it.* As a property it is cheaply queryable — org-ql filters
and sorts on properties directly — and drawer text is not. Moving it
trades an easy query for a parse. Duplicating buys one timeline and one
parser: analysis becomes "read the entries in order" rather than "read
a property, read a drawer, reconcile two timestamp conventions." It also
gives [[id:af7d3687-6d1e-4480-a9e2-df772785713f][af7d3687]]'s single
chronological drawer somewhere to begin.

*org already has the shape, so nothing needs inventing.* With no
previous state org renders the field as an empty string — =org.el= line
11092, =(or org-log-note-previous-state "")= — producing a native
creation entry with no previous state.

*Corrected 2026-08-11, twice, both by direct test rather than reading.*

1. *The literal above was wrong.* The empty string is padded, not
   quoted, so the real rendering carries no ="= pair at all:

   : - State "NEXT"       from              [2026-08-11 Tue 15:46]

   A parser keyed on =from ""= matches nothing. The claim that "existing
   parsers, and org-ql, handle it unmodified" was never verified and
   should be re-checked against that actual string before anything is
   built on it.

2. *=TODO= alone carried no =!= marker*, so the one transition this
   feature most needs — into =TODO=, the default keyword a capture
   receives — logged nothing, silently and without error. Interactivity
   is necessary for native logging but not sufficient; logging also has
   to be *requested*. Fixed by
   [[id:90a5a0cf-a81d-45dc-a453-0bfe61e3ef93][90a5a0cf]]. Had this
   shipped as written, it would have produced no creation entries for
   precisely the common case and looked like a bug in apply.

*The alternative, which may well be better and is not yet weighed:*
synthesise the creation entry *at read time*, in whatever reporting
function consumes this, from the =:CREATED:= property that already
exists. Nothing in any file changes, there is no duplication to diverge,
and no migration. The cost is that every consumer must know to do it,
where writing it once puts it in the record for anything that reads the
drawer — including a human. Decide this before writing anything.

*If the write-it-down option wins, two consequences:*
- *Backfill.* 56 headings have =:CREATED:= and no creation entry. A
  one-time pass can synthesise them from the property — mechanical and
  safe, with precedent in the retrospective consolidation pass recorded
  in DONE.org. Without it the analysis has a cutoff date.
- *It depends on [[id:cdcc065c-628d-4350-b1ba-fcf686e9c4c1][cdcc065c]].*
  =org_capture= does not write =:CREATED:= at all, so headings born from
  the tool have no creation time for a backfill to read. Fix that first
  or those headings have no origin in either place.

*Mechanism, if written at creation time:* it need not be special. Under
the queue model creation can simply *be* the =capture= event proposed in
[[id:7fa68d5c-b6c0-43df-85c6-764b1d56511f][7fa68d5c]], with apply
writing its =:LOGBOOK:= entry exactly as it writes a state transition.
The two headings are one feature approached from different sides —
capture durability there, timeline origin here.

*One semantic caveat for whoever writes the parser:* "created" and
"acquired its first TODO keyword" are not the same event in general.
They coincide throughout this project because every task heading is born
with a keyword, but the equivalence is a local accident, not a rule, and
a heading that gains a keyword later would make them diverge.

* Bigger swings

Significant directions this project has not committed to. Think hard
before building any of them. The first two are both about the
standalone/Warp-access subsystem from this session.

** MAYBE Decide what this module may change on a user's machine when installed :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       1caed585-3209-4108-8501-eba16baf6a01
:CREATED:  [2026-08-12 Wed 11:54]
:END:

Raised by the user 2026-08-12, from a narrow question — how to set
=org-log-into-drawer= permanently — that turned out to have a general
answer: *a distributable package configures its own behavior, and does
not silently reconfigure the user's.* Worth deciding once, because the
module already crosses that line in more than one place and each
crossing is cheap to fix now and expensive to fix after other people
depend on it.

*The specific inventory, as of 2026-08-12:*

1. *Four global hooks, installed unconditionally at load*
   (=config.el:1869-1873=): one =org-blocker-hook= and three
   =org-trigger-hook= functions. Anyone who enables =:tools
   claude-code-ide-org= gets auto-clock-in on every transition to
   =DOING=/=PLANNING=, =NEXT= demotion, and sole-=TODO= promotion
   applied to *every org file they open* — not only this project's.
   These are opinionated GTD policies, and they are the bigger problem
   here, not the smaller one.
2. *A hardcoded assumption about one user's config*
   (=config.el:2702-2708=): the comment reads "With =org-log-into-drawer=
   nil (this user's setting)". The =let=-binding it justifies is
   correct and should stay — binding locally rather than globally is
   exactly the right instinct, and it is already documented as
   deliberate — but the *default* wants to be a =defcustom= rather than
   an assumption about whoever wrote it.

*The precedent already set, same day.* The =org-log-into-drawer=
question was resolved by adding =#+STARTUP: logdrawer= to this repo's
own =TODO.org=/=DONE.org= headers rather than by =setq=-ing the
variable. Verified: =#+STARTUP:= sets it *buffer-locally*, leaving the
global untouched, so the scope claimed matches the scope owned. That is
the shape the rest of this should follow.

*The module is not starting from zero here.* Eight =defcustom=s already
exist (=-queue-directory=, =-capture-file=, =-working-hours=,
=-query-files=, =-session-recovery-enabled=, =-audit-log-file=,
=-clock-status-file=, =-guidepost-gap-threshold=). Configuring its own
behavior is established practice; the gap is the global hooks and the
one hardcoded assumption, not the general habit.

*Genuinely open, and the reason this is =MAYBE= rather than =TODO=:*
whether a *Doom module* earns more latitude than a *MELPA package*. A
Doom module is opted into explicitly, per user, by editing =init.el= —
which is arguably consent to exactly these policies, and the hooks are
much of why someone would enable it at all. A MELPA package is a
library, and the same behavior on load would be indefensible. The two
targets may want different answers, or one guarded entry point
(=claude-code-ide-org-setup=) that installs nothing until called. Decide
that before doing the work, since it determines whether the hooks move
behind a toggle or stay as they are.

*Related:* [[id:ab75d6d2-0e59-405d-92c6-2c67488db133][ab75d6d2]] changes
one of these very hooks, so whichever lands second should not re-litigate
the other's decision.
[[id:02aaae22-0958-4c0f-b5bb-2cbea32416ae][02aaae22]] owns the file-header
convention the =#+STARTUP:= line was added to.

** MAYBE Remote MCP access :code:
Off-machine access to the tools server and IDE-companion. This
session already got a =claude= CLI running directly in a Warp pane
on the same machine to reach Emacs (HTTP tools server +
IDE-companion lockfile, no vterm required). The obvious next step
is the same thing from *off-machine* — phone, another computer, a
CI job — but that means opening those servers beyond localhost,
which is real attack surface: arbitrary elisp execution against
your Emacs, today gated only by "it's on localhost." Don't build
this without designing auth first — bearer token at minimum, ideally
scoped per tool.

*Risk:* every registered tool call is unsandboxed elisp execution in
your live Emacs — a bearer token alone adds no TLS, per-tool
scoping, or rate limiting. Don't just flip the host to 0.0.0.0 and
call it done.

As a reusable snippet, not a private hack. Per the earlier scope

IDE-companion-autostart block currently lives in personal
config.el, hand-rolled against claude-code-ide's internals rather
than a public API. Short of a real upstream PR, even just
generalizing it (hardcoded project path → variable) and writing it
up would make it something worth handing to someone else.

*Open question:* the wrapped functions (=register-session=,
=mcp-start=) aren't really designed as public standalone-mode API
today, so an upstream PR is a bigger lift than it looks. Start with
the snippet, treat upstreaming as a separate future MAYBE.

*** TODO MCP HTTP server responds 200 instead of 202 Accepted to notifications :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       8f986c6f-7cab-42c5-9a21-01275f271c80
:CREATED:  [2026-07-30 Thu 12:21]

=claude-code-ide-mcp-http-server--send-empty-response= (the
id-less/notification response path, e.g. for
=notifications/initialized=) replies with HTTP =200= +
=Content-Type: text/plain=, but the MCP Streamable HTTP spec expects
=202 Accepted= for that case. Confirmed live, found while bridging
Warp to this server: Warp's own MCP client fails with "Transport
channel closed" sending =notifications/initialized= against the
unpatched server; the Python =mcp= SDK's client (used by
=mcp-proxy=) instead rejects the same response with "Unexpected
content type: text/plain". Verified fix: changing just the status
code to =202= (confirmed transiently via =emacsclient= before being
persisted as a Doom config override) resolves both — a full
=tools/list= round-trip succeeded afterward. Worked around locally in
this project's Doom config pending an upstream report/PR.

:PROPERTIES:

No README.md exists in this repo today — CLAUDE.md is currently the
*only* documentation file, doing double duty as both agent
instructions and de facto human-facing intro (confirmed: this repo has
no other .md file at all). Decided against Claude Code's =@README.md=
import syntax inside CLAUDE.md (which would auto-load the full file
into every session's context, same as CLAUDE.md itself) in favor of a
small, deliberate amount of redundancy: a human-facing README.md
(intro/tutorial framing, quickstart, maybe a trimmed install summary)
kept separate from CLAUDE.md, accepting that the small overlap zone
(mainly the intro/description, per CLAUDE.md's own opening lines and
maybe an abbreviated version of its "Emacs integration" section) needs
manual syncing rather than paying the token cost of importing the
whole file every session.

Two parts:
1. Write README.md itself.
2. Add a =PostToolUse= hook (matching =Edit|Write=, filtered to fire
   only when =tool_input.file_path= is =CLAUDE.md= or =README.md=,
   read via =jq= from the hook's stdin JSON — same idiom as
   =bin/hooks/pretooluse-transition-guard=) that injects an
   =additionalContext= reminder to check the sibling file for anything
   that also needs updating. Mirrors the existing =SessionStart=
   stale-interval-recovery hook's pattern (inject context, let Claude
   relay/act on it) rather than hard-blocking the edit, which would be
   heavier-handed than this warrants.

Revisit 2026-07-31.

*Note added 2026-08-05:* consider a "Known limitations" section in the
README, surfacing accepted-but-easy-to-miss gaps that today live only
as prose buried in CLAUDE.md — cited here by content, not line number,
since line numbers drift (confirmed: this note's original citations
were already off by ~10 lines within the same day). Two examples, both
deliberately left as low-cost/self-correcting rather than fixed, from
CLAUDE.md's "State transition rules" section:
- *"Known gap, accepted"*: the =ExitPlanMode= hook fires whether a plan
  was approved or rejected, with no reliable signal to tell the two
  apart, so a rejected plan can still trigger a stray =PLANNING= →
  =DOING= promotion.
- *"Cross-session guard"*: the same promotion only fires for the
  session that set =PLANNING= in the first place, tracked via
  =claude-code-ide-org--planning-owner-session-id=.

A human skimming the repo has no reason to find either today unless
they read that CLAUDE.md section end to end.

** TODO emacs is a shell function that silently breaks inline batch verification :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       4f9e552a-e1a9-48dd-8801-3f23a31b9e32
:CREATED:  [2026-08-11 Tue 15:52]
:END:

The user's shell profile defines:

: emacs () { if [[ "$*" == *-nw* ]]; then command emacs "$@"; else open -a Emacs --args "$@"; fi }

Any =emacs --batch ...= run from an interactive-profile shell therefore
launches the GUI app and returns immediately: exit 0, no stdout, no
stderr. Indistinguishable from a clean run that found nothing. Hit three
times in a row on 2026-08-11 before being noticed, and nearly recorded
as a genuine "org logs nothing" finding.

=bin/test= is unaffected — a =#!/usr/bin/env bash= script gets a
non-interactive shell where the function is not defined, so =exec emacs=
resolves to =/opt/homebrew/bin/emacs=. The trap is specific to inline
one-off verification, which is exactly where the CLAUDE.md rule about
stating the reload precondition is meant to apply.

Fix is a convention, not code: use =command emacs= or the absolute path
for any batch invocation. Worth a line in the org-dev skill, which
exists for exactly this class of "did the change take effect" check.
